--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled K and N. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node K to find the shortest path to node N.

1.  **Level 0:** Start at node K.
    *   Current path: `[K]`

2.  **Level 1:** Explore neighbors of K.
    *   K is connected to X, F, G.
    *   Paths: `[K, X]`, `[K, F]`, `[K, G]`

3.  **Level 2:** Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 1 (excluding K, X, F, G which are already visited or in the current level).
    *   From X: No new unvisited neighbors that lead to a shorter path than already explored.
    *   From F: F is connected to E.
        *   Path: `[K, F, E]`
    *   From G: G is connected to H.
        *   Path: `[K, G, H]`

4.  **Level 3:** Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 2 (excluding K, X, F, G, E, H which are already visited or in the current level).
    *   From E: E is connected to L, M, I, B.
        *   Paths: `[K, F, E, L]`, `[K, F, E, M]`, `[K, F, E, I]`, `[K, F, E, B]`
    *   From H: H is connected to B, I.
        *   Paths: `[K, G, H, B]`, `[K, G, H, I]`

5.  **Level 4:** Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 3 (excluding already visited nodes).
    *   From L: No new unvisited neighbors.
    *   From M: M is connected to J.
        *   Path: `[K, F, E, M, J]`
    *   From I: I is connected to C, J, P.
        *   Paths: `[K, F, E, I, C]`, `[K, F, E, I, J]`, `[K, F, E, I, P]` (and similar from `[K, G, H, I]`)
    *   From B: B is connected to C, N.
        *   B is connected to N, which is our target node!
        *   Path from `[K, F, E, B]`: `[K, F, E, B, N]` (Length 4)
        *   Path from `[K, G, H, B]`: `[K, G, H, B, N]` (Length 4)

We have found the target node N at Level 4. The shortest path length is 4.
One such path is `[K, F, E, B, N]`.
Let's verify the connections for this path:
*   Is K directly connected to F? Yes.
*   Is F directly connected to E? Yes.
*   Is E directly connected to B? Yes.
*   Is B directly connected to N? Yes.

This path is valid. Since BFS explores level by level, the first time the target node is reached guarantees the shortest path.

Final Answer: Length: 4, Path: [K, F, E, B, N]

Image name: SEIS_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G and N. I cannot remove G or N.

2.  **Analyze connections to N:** Node N is directly connected to nodes B, C, and Y.
    *   Any path from G to N must pass through at least one of these three nodes (B, C, Y).

3.  **Analyze node Y's role:** Node Y is connected to N and C. This means any path from G to N that involves Y must necessarily pass through C first (e.g., G -> ... -> C -> Y -> N). Therefore, if C is removed, Y becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except N), and no path can reach N via Y from G.

4.  **Simplify the problem:** Based on the analysis of Y, we can conclude that to disconnect N from the rest of the graph (and thus from G), we only need to sever all paths leading to B and C. If B and C are removed, N will only be connected to Y, but Y itself will be isolated from any path originating from G because its only other connection (C) is also removed.

5.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If I remove B: Paths like G-H-I-C-N or G-F-M-J-P-O-C-N would still exist. So, removing B alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove C: Paths like G-H-B-N or G-F-E-B-N would still exist. So, removing C alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove Y: Paths through B and C would still exist. So, removing Y alone is not enough.
    *   Since no single node removal disconnects G and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

6.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    *   Consider removing nodes B and C.
        *   All paths from G that lead to N via B (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-F-E-B-N) would be broken because B is removed.
        *   All paths from G that lead to N via C (e.g., G-H-I-C-N, G-F-M-J-P-O-C-N) would be broken because C is removed.
        *   As established in step 3, any path involving Y would also be broken because Y's only connection to the G-side of the graph is through C.
    *   Therefore, removing both B and C successfully disconnects G and N.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is insufficient, and a two-node removal (B and C) is sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, C]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['E', 'H']}

Image name: Tu_task2_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and J, without removing E or J themselves.

I will systematically identify all paths between E and J and then determine the critical nodes that must be removed to break these paths.

Let's list the direct neighbors of E and J:
Neighbors of E: L, M, P, B
Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I

I will categorize the paths based on the first node they pass through from E, or the last node they pass through before reaching J.

**Category 1: Paths through M**
1.  E - M - J
2.  E - L - M - J
To break these paths, node M must be removed.

**Category 2: Paths through P**
1.  E - P - J
2.  E - B - C - N - O - P - J
3.  E - B - C - O - P - J
4.  E - B - C - N - Y - O - P - J
To break these paths, node P must be removed.

**Category 3: Paths through B and then I (or H then I)**
1.  E - B - C - I - J
2.  E - B - C - H - I - J
3.  E - B - H - I - J
To break these paths, nodes B or I (or C or H) must be removed. However, I is the common node for all these paths just before J.

Let's test for a cut set of size 1:
- If M is removed: Paths E-P-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J (and others through B) still exist. Not enough.
- If P is removed: Paths E-M-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J (and others through B) still exist. Not enough.
- If B is removed: Paths E-M-J, E-P-J still exist. Not enough.
- If I is removed: Paths E-M-J, E-P-J still exist. Not enough.
Since no single node can disconnect E and J, the minimum cut set size is greater than 1.

Let's test for a cut set of size 2. I will consider combinations of the critical nodes identified (M, P, and I, which is a bottleneck for Category 3 paths).

- **Remove M and P**:
    - Paths E-M-J and E-P-J are broken.
    - However, paths like E - B - H - I - J and E - B - C - I - J still exist. So, [M, P] is not enough.

- **Remove M and I**:
    - Paths E-M-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J (and others through I) are broken.
    - However, the path E - P - J still exists. So, [M, I] is not enough.

- **Remove P and I**:
    - Paths E-P-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J (and others through I and P) are broken.
    - However, the path E - M - J still exists. So, [P, I] is not enough.

Since removing any two of the key bottleneck nodes (M, P, I) leaves at least one major path group intact, a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient.

Let's try a cut set of size 3.
Consider removing the set of nodes {M, P, I}.

1.  **Removing M**: Breaks all paths in Category 1 (E-M-J, E-L-M-J).
2.  **Removing P**: Breaks all paths in Category 2 (E-P-J, E-B-C-N-O-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-C-N-Y-O-P-J).
3.  **Removing I**: Breaks all paths in Category 3 (E-B-C-I-J, E-B-C-H-I-J, E-B-H-I-J).

Let's verify that no path remains from E to J after removing M, P, and I:
- From E, paths can go to L, M, P, B.
    - E-L: L is connected only to M. M is removed. So this branch is cut.
    - E-M: M is removed. This branch is cut.
    - E-P: P is removed. This branch is cut.
    - E-B:
        - E-B-C:
            - C-N: N is connected to Y, O. O is connected to P. P is removed. Cut.
            - C-O: O is connected to P. P is removed. Cut.
            - C-H: H is connected to I. I is removed. Cut.
            - C-I: I is removed. Cut.
        - E-B-H: H is connected to I. I is removed. Cut.

All possible paths from E to J are broken by removing M, P, and I.
Since a cut set of size 2 is not possible, and a cut set of size 3 has been found, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, P, I]

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are J and L.
2.  **Identify direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Node J is directly connected to D, P, M, and I.
    *   Node L is directly connected to M and E.
3.  **Analyze paths based on immediate connections:** Any path from J to L must pass through one of J's neighbors and one of L's neighbors.
    *   **Paths through M:**
        *   J -> M -> L (direct path)
        *   J -> M -> E -> L
    *   **Paths not through M:** If a path does not use M, it must go from J to one of {D, P, I} and then eventually reach E, and then L.
        *   J -> D -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E -> L
        *   J -> D -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> E -> L
        *   J -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E -> L
        *   J -> P -> O -> Y -> N -> B -> E -> L
        *   J -> I -> H -> C -> B -> E -> L
        *   J -> I -> H -> G -> X -> K -> F -> E -> L
        *   J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> F -> E -> L
        *   J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> X -> F -> E -> L

4.  **Evaluate single-node cuts:**
    *   If we remove M: The path J -> M -> L is broken, and J -> M -> E -> L is broken. However, all paths that go through E (e.g., J -> D -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E -> L) still exist. So, removing M alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove E: All paths that go through E (e.g., J -> D -> P -> O -> N -> B -> E -> L) are broken. However, the path J -> M -> L still exists. So, removing E alone is not sufficient.
    *   Removing any other single node (D, P, I, O, N, B, C, H, G, X, K, F, Y) would leave at least one path through M or E intact. For example, removing B would still leave J -> M -> L and J -> I -> H -> G -> K -> F -> E -> L.

5.  **Evaluate two-node cuts:**
    *   Consider the set of nodes {M, E}.
        *   If M is removed, all paths that directly use M (J -> M -> L and J -> M -> E -> L) are broken.
        *   After M is removed, any remaining path from J to L must necessarily go through E (since E is the only other node directly connected to L).
        *   If E is also removed, then L becomes completely isolated from J (as both M and E, its only connections to the rest of the graph, are removed).
        *   Therefore, removing both M and E successfully disconnects J and L.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since a single node removal is not sufficient, and we found a set of two nodes that disconnects J and L, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [M, E]

2.  **Identify Immediate Connections:**
    *   Neighbors of O: P, Y, N, M, C
    *   Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G
3.  **Analyze Paths and Bottlenecks:** I will systematically explore all paths from O to H and identify critical nodes whose removal would sever these paths.

    *   **Path Group 1: Direct connection through C.**
        There is a direct path: O - C - H.
        Additionally, paths like O - N - B - C - H and O - Y - N - B - C - H also pass through C.
        Removing node **C** would break all these paths.

    *   **Path Group 2: Paths to H via I.**
        All paths from O to H that go through node I must pass through J. Let's list some examples:
        *   O - P - D - J - I - H
        *   O - M - J - I - H
        *   O - N - B - E - L - M - J - I - H
        *   O - Y - N - B - E - L - M - J - I - H
        In all these paths, **J** is a common node before reaching I. Removing node **J** would break all paths from O to I (and thus to H).

    *   **Path Group 3: Paths to H via X or G.**
        All paths from O to H that go through nodes X or G seem to converge through nodes E and F. Let's list some examples:
        *   O - N - B - E - F - X - H
        *   O - N - B - E - F - K - X - H
        *   O - N - B - E - F - G - H
        *   O - N - B - E - F - K - G - H
        *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - X - H
        *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - K - X - H
        *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - G - H
        *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - K - G - H
        *   O - M - L - E - F - X - H
        *   O - M - L - E - F - K - X - H
        *   O - M - L - E - F - G - H
        *   O - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
        All these paths pass through both E and F. Therefore, removing either **E** or **F** would break all these paths.

4.  **Forming a Cut Set:**
    To disconnect O and H, we need to break paths from all three groups.
    *   From Group 1, we need to remove C.
    *   From Group 2, we need to remove J.
    *   From Group 3, we need to remove E (or F).
    This leads to a cut set of size 3, for example: [C, J, E].

5.  **Verifying Minimality:**
    Let's check if a cut set of size 2 is sufficient.
    *   If we remove [C, J]: Paths like O - N - B - E - F - X - H would still exist.
    *   If we remove [C, E]: Paths like O - P - D - J - I - H would still exist.
    *   If we remove [J, E]: The direct path O - C - H would still exist.
    Since no combination of two nodes can disconnect O and H, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and I (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of G and I:**
    *   Neighbors of G: E, F, H, B
    *   Neighbors of I: H, P, C, O

2.  **Explore possible paths between G and I:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I. According to Menger's theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        Edges: {(G, H), (H, I)}

    *   **Path 2:** G - B - C - I
        Edges: {(G, B), (B, C), (C, I)}
        (These edges are distinct from Path 1's edges.)

    *   **Path 3:** G - E - L - M - J - P - I
        Edges: {(G, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I)}
        (These edges are distinct from Path 1 and Path 2's edges.)

    *   **Path 4:** G - F - K - J - O - I
        Edges: {(G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, O), (O, I)}

    *   Path 2: {(G, B), (B, C), (C, I)}

    *   Path 4: {(G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, O), (O, I)}
    All four paths use entirely different sets of edges. Therefore, there are at least 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I. This implies that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect G from I is at least 4.

4.  **Identify a cut set of 4 edges:**
    Since the minimum cut is at least 4, I need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal disconnects G from I. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. Node I has four direct connections: (H, I), (P, I), (C, I), and (O, I).

    Let's propose the cut set: [(H, I), (P, I), (C, I), (O, I)].

First, I will attempt to find node-disjoint paths between H and O. Node-disjoint means that these paths do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and O).

1.  **Path 1: H - B - N - Y - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {B, N, Y}

2.  **Path 2: H - I - C - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {I, C}

3.  **Path 3: H - G - K - J - D - P - O**
    Intermediate nodes: {G, K, J, D, P}

Let's verify that these three paths are indeed node-disjoint:
*   Path 1 uses nodes B, N, Y.
*   Path 2 uses nodes I, C.
*   Path 3 uses nodes G, K, J, D, P.
All intermediate nodes in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, I have found 3 node-disjoint paths between H and O.

According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 3 node-disjoint paths, then at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H and O.

Next, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal successfully disconnects H and O.
Consider the nodes directly connected to H: B, G, I.
If we remove these three nodes, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from O.
Let's verify this cut set:
*   If node B is removed, all paths starting with H-B are severed.
*   If node G is removed, all paths starting with H-G are severed.
*   If node I is removed, all paths starting with H-I are severed.
Since all paths from H must start through B, G, or I, removing these three nodes will disconnect H from O.
Thus, [B, G, I] is a valid cut set of size 3.

Alternatively, consider the nodes directly connected to O: C, P, Y.
If we remove these three nodes, O will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from H.
Let's verify this cut set:
*   If node C is removed, all paths ending with C-O are severed.
*   If node P is removed, all paths ending with P-O are severed.
*   If node Y is removed, all paths ending with Y-O are severed.
Since all paths to O must end through C, P, or Y, removing these three nodes will disconnect H from O.
Thus, [C, P, Y] is also a valid cut set of size 3.

Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3. And I have identified a cut set of size 3. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, G, I] or [C, P, Y]

    Finding edge-disjoint paths helps establish a lower bound for the minimum cut.
    *   Let's find the first path: P1 = B - H - E - M. The edges are {(B, H), (H, E), (E, M)}.
    *   Now, let's find a second path that does not use any of the edges from P1.
        P2 = B - C - I - J - M. The edges are {(B, C), (C, I), (I, J), (J, M)}.
        Comparing P1 and P2, they do not share any common edges. Thus, P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   Since I can only find two edge-disjoint paths (P1 and P2), the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

    Consider the edges that connect to M: (E, M), (L, M), (J, M).

Neighbors of O: P, Y, N, M
Neighbors of H: I, C, X, G

**1. Paths to H via Node I:**
   - Path 1: O - P - D - J - I - H
   - Path 2: O - M - J - I - H
   Both of these paths converge at node J and then proceed to I. The edge (J, I) is common to both paths leading to I. If we remove the edge (J, I), all paths from O to I (and subsequently to H via I) are broken, as I is only connected to J and H from the perspective of reaching it from O.

**2. Paths to H via Node C:**
   - Path 3: O - P - Y - N - C - H
   - Path 4: O - N - B - C - H
   Both of these paths converge at node N and then proceed to C. The edge (N, C) is common to both paths leading to C. If we remove the edge (N, C), all paths from O to C (and subsequently to H via C) are broken, as C's connections to O are exclusively through N (via Y or B).

**3. Paths to H via Node X:**
   - Path 5: O - N - B - E - F - X - H
   - Path 6: O - N - B - E - F - K - X - H
   - Path 7: O - M - L - E - F - X - H
   - Path 8: O - M - L - E - F - K - X - H
   All these paths pass through node F and then to X. The edge (F, X) is common to all paths leading to X. If we remove the edge (F, X), all paths from O to X (and subsequently to H via X) are broken.

   - For example: O - N - B - E - F - X - G - H.
   Since all paths from O to G must pass through X, and we have identified that removing (F, X) disconnects X from O, it follows that G will also be disconnected from O. Therefore, no additional edge needs to be removed specifically for paths through G if (F, X) is already removed.

Based on this analysis, we can identify a set of three edges that, when removed, will disconnect O from H:
- **(J, I)**: This cuts all paths from O to I.
- **(N, C)**: This cuts all paths from O to C.
- **(F, X)**: This cuts all paths from O to X, and consequently, all paths from O to G (since G is only connected to X and H from O's perspective).

1. Removing (J, I) ensures no path can reach H via I.
2. Removing (N, C) ensures no path can reach H via C.
3. Removing (F, X) ensures no path can reach H via X. It also ensures no path can reach H via G, because G is only connected to X (and H), and X is now disconnected from O.

Thus, all possible routes from O to H are severed. This cut set has 3 edges.

Could we do it with fewer than 3 edges?
- If we remove only 1 edge, there are clearly multiple independent paths remaining.
- If we remove only 2 edges:
    - Removing (J, I) and (N, C) would leave paths through X and G open (e.g., O-N-B-E-F-X-H).
    - Removing (J, I) and (F, X) would leave paths through C open (e.g., O-N-B-C-H).
    - Removing (N, C) and (F, X) would leave paths through I open (e.g., O-M-J-I-H).

    *   Neighbors of I: E, H, B, C, J, P

        *   G - H - I (direct path)

        *   G - H - B - C - I
        *   G - H - B - C - N - O - P - I

    *   **Channel 2: Through F (and then E)**
        G is connected to F via the edge (G, F). Additionally, G is connected to K and X, both of which are connected to F. So, all paths from G that do not go through H must eventually pass through F. From F, the primary way to reach I is through E:
        *   G - F - E - I (direct path)
        *   G - F - E - L - M - J - I
        *   G - F - E - L - M - J - P - I
        *   G - F - E - M - J - I
        *   G - F - E - M - J - P - I
        Similarly, paths starting G-K-F-... or G-X-F-... also converge at F and then proceed via E.
        All these paths rely on the edge (F, E) to connect the F-subgraph to the E-subgraph, which then leads to I.

3.  **Identify potential cut edges:**
    Based on the analysis above, it appears that all paths from G to I must either use the edge (G, H) or pass through F and then use the edge (F, E).

    Let's propose a cut set: [(G, H), (F, E)].

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   If (G, H) is removed: All paths starting from G and going through H are broken. This includes G-H-I, G-H-B-I, G-H-B-C-I, etc.
    *   If (F, E) is removed: All paths that go from G to F (either directly G-F, or indirectly G-K-F, G-X-F) will be unable to proceed from F to E.     Therefore, removing both (G, H) and (F, E) ensures that no path exists between G and I.

5.  **Determine minimality:**
    To prove minimality, we need to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I.

    *   Path 2: G - F - E - I (Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, I))

    *   Node N is connected to Y, O, B.
    *   Node G is connected to X, K, H.
    Removing all edges connected to N (i.e., (N,Y), (N,O), (N,B)) would disconnect N from the graph, requiring 3 edges.
    Removing all edges connected to G (i.e., (G,X), (G,K), (G,H)) would disconnect G from the graph, requiring 3 edges.
    This suggests the minimum cut is at most 3.

3.  **Attempt to find a cut of size 1:**
    A cut of size 1 would mean there is a bridge edge whose removal disconnects N and G. By visual inspection, there are multiple paths between N and G, so no single edge removal will suffice. For example, removing (H,G) would still leave paths through K and X to G. Thus, the minimum cut is greater than 1.

4.  **Attempt to find a cut of size 2 using Menger's Theorem (edge-disjoint paths):**
    Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from N to G.

    *   **Path 1:** N - B - E - F - K - G
        Edges: {(N,B), (B,E), (E,F), (F,K), (K,G)}

    *   **Path 2:** N - O - P - J - I - H - G
        Edges: {(N,O), (O,P), (P,J), (J,I), (I,H), (H,G)}

    These two paths (Path 1 and Path 2) are completely edge-disjoint. They do not share any common edges.

    *   **Attempt to find a Path 3 that is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:**
        Path 1 uses the edge (N,B). Path 2 uses the edge (N,O).
        The only remaining edge connected to N is (N,Y). So, Path 3 must start with (N,Y).
        Path 3: N - Y ...
        From Y, the only connection is to O.
        Path 3: N - Y - O ...
        Now, from O, to continue towards G, O has connections to N, Y, and P.
        (O,N) is used in Path 2. (O,Y) is used in Path 3.
        The only remaining edge from O that leads away from N/Y is (O,P).
        So, Path 3 would have to be N - Y - O - P ...
        However, the edge (O,P) is already used in Path 2.
        Since any path starting with (N,Y) must eventually pass through (O,P) to reach G (without looping back to N or Y), it will share an edge with Path 2.
        Therefore, it is not possible to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

    This implies that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between N and G is 2. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N and G is 2.

5.  **Identify a cut set of 2 edges:**
    Based on the analysis of edge-disjoint paths, we need to find two edges whose removal breaks all paths.
    Consider the edges (N,B) and (O,P).

    *   Any path starting from N via (N,B) will be cut by removing (N,B).
    *   Any path starting from N via (N,O) will proceed to O, and then must use (O,P) to move towards G (as N and Y are the only other connections from O). So, N-O-P... will be cut by removing (O,P).
    *   Any path starting from N via (N,Y) will proceed to Y, then to O (Y's only connection). From O, it must use (O,P) to move towards G. So, N-Y-O-P... will be cut by removing (O,P).

Now, let's categorize the paths from G to I:

**Category 1: Paths through H**
The most direct path is G - H - I.
To cut this path, we can remove either edge (G, H) or (H, I).

**Category 2: Paths not through H**
If a path from G to I does not use H, it must start from G and go through one of its other neighbors: K, F, or X.
*   If it goes G-K, it must then go to F or X.
*   If it goes G-X, it must then go to F or K.
*   If it goes G-F, it can then go to P.
In all these cases (G-K-F, G-X-F, G-F), the path eventually reaches node F.
From node F, the only way to reach I (without using H, which is already covered in Category 1) is through node P. F is connected to P.
From P, there are two ways to reach I:
    *   Directly: P - I
    *   Via J: P - J - I

So, all paths in Category 2 must pass through the edge (F, P). Examples include:
*   G - F - P - I
*   G - F - P - J - I
*   G - K - F - P - I
*   G - K - F - P - J - I
*   G - X - F - P - I
*   G - X - F - P - J - I

To disconnect G and I, we must cut at least one edge from Category 1 paths and at least one edge from Category 2 paths.

To cut Category 1 paths most efficiently, we can remove either (G, H) or (H, I).
To cut Category 2 paths most efficiently, we can remove the edge (F, P), as all these paths converge on this edge to reach P, and then I.

Therefore, a minimal cut set will consist of two edges: one from {(G, H), (H, I)} and the edge (F, P).
This gives us two possible minimal cut sets of size 2:
1.  [(H, I), (F, P)]
2.  [(G, H), (F, P)]

Let's verify the first option: [(H, I), (F, P)].
*   Removing (H, I) breaks the path G-H-I.
*   Removing (F, P) breaks all paths that go through F and P to reach I (e.g., G-F-P-I, G-F-P-J-I, G-K-F-P-I, G-X-F-P-I, etc.).
With these two edges removed, there are no remaining paths between G and I.

Since we found a cut set of size 2, and it's clear that a single edge removal would not disconnect G and I (as there are at least two independent "arteries" of paths: one through H and one through F-P), the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (F, P)]

The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and J.

- Node E is connected to: M, L, F, B.
- Node J is connected to: M, I, P, D.

    *   This path can be cut by removing edge (E, M) or (M, J).
2.  E - L - M - J:
    *   This path can be cut by removing edge (E, L), (L, M), or (M, J).
    *   Note that both paths in this category rely on the connection (M, J) to reach J.

**Category 2: Paths starting E - F**
1.  E - F - G - H - I - J:
    *   This path can be cut by removing (E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), or (I, J).
2.  E - F - X - K - G - H - I - J:
    *   This path can be cut by removing (E, F), (F, X), (X, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, I), or (I, J).
    *   Note that both paths in this category rely on the connection (G, H) to move from the 'left' side of the graph (containing E, F, G, X, K) to the 'right' side (containing H, I, J).

**Category 3: Paths starting E - B**
This category includes several sub-paths, as B is connected to H, C, and N.
1.  E - B - H - I - J
2.  E - B - C - I - J
3.  E - B - C - N - O - P - J
4.  E - B - C - O - P - J
5.  E - B - N - O - P - J
6.  E - B - N - C - I - J
7.  E - B - N - C - O - P - J
8.  E - B - C - O - P - D - J (Note: D is connected to P and J, so this is a valid path)
    *   All paths in this category rely on the initial connection (E, B) to move from E to the rest of the graph that leads to J.

**Identifying a Minimal Cut Set:**

I observe that the graph can be conceptually divided into two main "blocks" or regions:
-   **Left Block (E-side):** Contains nodes {E, M, L, F, X, K, G}.
-   **Right Block (J-side):** Contains nodes {J, D, P, I, O, Y, N, C, H, B}.

To disconnect E from J, I need to cut all edges that bridge these two blocks. I identify the following edges that connect a node in the Left Block to a node in the Right Block, or directly to J:

1.  **(M, J):** This edge connects M (from the Left Block, reachable from E via E-M or E-L-M) directly to J (in the Right Block).
2.  **(G, H):** This edge connects G (from the Left Block, reachable from E via E-F-G or E-F-X-K-G) to H (in the Right Block).
3.  **(E, B):** This edge connects E (from the Left Block) directly to B (in the Right Block).

Let's propose the cut set: **[(M, J), (G, H), (E, B)]**. This set contains 3 edges.

**Verification of the proposed cut set:**

1.  **Removing (M, J):**
    *   This breaks all paths from E that go through M to reach J, specifically E-M-J and E-L-M-J.

2.  **Removing (G, H):**
    *   This breaks all paths from E that go through F and G to reach H and then J, specifically E-F-G-H-I-J and E-F-X-K-G-H-I-J. After removing (G, H), node G (and thus F, X, K, L, M, E) cannot reach H, I, P, D, J.

3.  **Removing (E, B):**
    *   This breaks all paths from E that go through B to reach H, C, N, and subsequently I, P, D, J. This includes all paths listed under Category 3.

After removing these three edges, E is only connected to L, M, F, X, K, G. None of these nodes can reach J because:
-   M's connection to J (M, J) is cut.
-   G's connection to H (G, H) is cut, preventing paths through H, I, P, D to J.
-   E's connection to B (E, B) is cut, preventing paths through B, C, N, O, P, D, I to J.

Therefore, E is completely disconnected from J. The number of edges in this cut set is 3.

**Proving Minimality:**

To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that removing any two of these three edges would leave at least one path between E and J.
-   If (M, J) and (G, H) are removed, the edge (E, B) remains. A path like E-B-C-O-P-J (or any other path starting E-B) would still exist.
-   If (M, J) and (E, B) are removed, the edge (G, H) remains. A path like E-F-G-H-I-J would still exist.
-   If (G, H) and (E, B) are removed, the edge (M, J) remains. A path like E-M-J would still exist.

Since removing any two edges from this set leaves a path, all three edges are necessary to disconnect E from J. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (G, H), (J, M)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G and N.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node G is connected to E, F, H, X.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, Y.

2.  **Consider a simple cut strategy:** To disconnect a node from the rest of the graph, one can remove all edges directly connected to it.
    *   Removing all edges connected to N: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y). This would be a cut set of size 3.
    *   Removing all edges connected to G: (G, E), (G, F), (G, H), (G, X). This would be a cut set of size 4.
    The cut set of size 3 is smaller, so the minimum is at most 3.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least 3.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from G to N:**
    *   **Path 1:** G - E - B - N
        Edges: {(G, E), (E, B), (B, N)}
    *   **Path 2:** G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - K - J - P - O - Y - N
        Edges: {(G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)}

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   Comparing Path 1 and Path 2: All edges are distinct. (G,E) != (G,H), (E,B) != (H,I), (B,N) != (I,C) and (B,N) != (C,N).
    *   Comparing Path 1 and Path 3: All edges are distinct. (G,E) != (G,F), (E,B) != (F,K), (B,N) != (Y,N).
    *   Comparing Path 2 and Path 3: All edges are distinct. (G,H) != (G,F), (H,I) != (F,K), (I,C) != (K,J), (C,N) != (Y,N).
    Since all three paths use entirely different sets of edges, they are indeed edge-disjoint.

6.  **Determine the minimum cut size:** Because we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3. Since we also identified a cut set of size 3 (removing all edges directly connected to N), this is the minimum possible.

7.  **Final Cut Set:** The set of edges [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)] effectively disconnects N from the rest of the graph, and thus from G, by removing all its outgoing/incoming connections.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, Y)]

    *   Visited: `{D}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Current node: D
    *   Path: `[D]`

3.  **Level 1:**
    *   Dequeue `(D, [D])`.

    *   Enqueue `(J, [D, J])`. Mark J as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(P, [D, P])`. Mark P as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(J, [D, J]), (P, [D, P])]`
    *   Visited: `{D, J, P}`

4.  **Level 2:**

    *   Neighbors of J: D (visited), M, I.
    *   Enqueue `(M, [D, J, M])`. Mark M as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(I, [D, J, I])`. Mark I as visited.

    *   Visited: `{D, J, P, M, I, O}`

5.  **Level 3:**
    *   Dequeue `(M, [D, J, M])`.
    *   Neighbors of M: J (visited), L, E.
    *   Enqueue `(L, [D, J, M, L])`. Mark L as visited.
    *   Enqueue `(E, [D, J, M, E])`. Mark E as visited.

    *   Enqueue `(H, [D, J, I, H])`. Mark H as visited.

    *   Visited: `{D, J, P, M, I, O, L, E, C, H, Y}`

6.  **Level 4:**

    *   Dequeue `(E, [D, J, M, E])`.

    *   **Node F is the target node!**
    *   The path found is `[D, J, M, E, F]`.
    *   The length of this path is 4.

*   Is J directly connected to M? Yes.

    *   **From G's side:** The nodes G, K, X, F form a local cluster. The only edges connecting this cluster to the rest of the graph (and thus towards N) are (G, E), (G, H), and (F, E). *   M -> J -> C -> I -> H
All these paths rely on node J to proceed towards H.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify direct connections and common neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of E: L, M, P, B
    *   Neighbors of J: M, D, P, I
    *   Common neighbors: M, P. This means there are direct paths E-M-J and E-P-J.

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum number of edges to remove (edge connectivity), I look for edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J. (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J. (Edges: (E, P), (P, J))
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - H - I - J. (Edges: (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J))

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1 uses edges (E, M) and (M, J).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (E, P) and (P, J).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), and (I, J).
    All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (edge connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and J.
    Consider the edges directly connected to J that lead towards E: (J, M), (J, P), (J, I). The edge (J, D) leads to a dead end for paths to E.
    Let's propose the cut set: **[(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]**.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   Removing (J, M) breaks the path E-M-J.
    *   Removing (J, P) breaks the path E-P-J. It also breaks any other path that uses P to reach J, such as E-B-H-G-F-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, and E-B-C-N-O-P-J.
    *   Removing (J, I) breaks any path that uses I to reach J, such as E-B-C-I-J and E-B-H-I-J.

    By removing these three edges, J is completely isolated from any path originating from E. All paths identified (and any others) are broken.

Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of 3 edges, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from B.

1.  **Identify direct connection:** Nodes H and B are directly connected by the edge (H, B). This edge must be removed as part of any cut set.

2.  **Identify all paths (after removing the direct edge):**
    After removing (H, B), I need to find all alternative paths between H and B.
    Let's list the neighbors of H: G, I.
    Let's list the neighbors of B (excluding H): E, G, C, N.

    All paths from H to B must start with either (H, G) or (H, I).
    All paths from B to H must end with an edge connected to B from E, G, C, or N.

    Let's trace paths:
    *   **Paths through G from H:**
        *   H - G - B (Path 2)
        *   H - G - F - E - B (Path 3)
    *   **Paths through I from H:**
        *   H - I - C - B (Path 4)
        *   H - I - P - O - C - B (Path 5)
        *   H - I - P - J - M - E - B (Path 6)

3.  **Find edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum cut, I can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and B (Menger's Theorem).

    *   **Path 1:** H - B
        Edges: {(H, B)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - G - B
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, B)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - C - B
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B:
    1.  P1: H - B
    2.  P2: H - G - B
    3.  P3: H - I - C - B

    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

4.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and B.
    Consider the edges directly connected to H: (H, B), (H, G), (H, I).
    If I remove these three edges:
    *   (H, B) breaks the direct path.
    *   (H, G) breaks all paths starting from H through G (e.g., H-G-B, H-G-F-E-B).
    *   (H, I) breaks all paths starting from H through I (e.g., H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-J-M-E-B).

    By removing these three edges, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from node B.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    The cut set [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)] successfully disconnects H from B, and it contains 3 edges. Since I established that at least 3 edges are required, this is the minimum number.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]

3.  **Analyze paths to D:** Any path from E to D must, by definition, pass through one of D's direct neighbors as its final step. Therefore, every path from E to D must include either node J or node P (or both, if a path goes E -> ... -> J -> P -> D, which is not possible as D is the target).
4.  **Consider removing a single node:**

    *   If I remove only node P, paths like E -> ... -> J -> D would still exist, connecting E and D.

5.  **Consider removing two nodes:** If I remove both node J and node P, then D will have no remaining connections to any other nodes in the graph. Consequently, no path from E (or any other node) to D can exist.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since removing one node is insufficient, and removing two nodes (J and P) successfully disconnects E from D, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. The cut set consists of J and P.

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J (black) and F (black). The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J from F. This is a minimum (J, F)-cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between J and F.

First, let's identify all edges connected to node J:
1. (J, M)
2. (J, D)
3. (J, P)
4. (J, I)

If we remove all these 4 edges, node J becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from J to F. This would be a cut set of size 4.

Now, let's try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between J and F to confirm if 4 is indeed the minimum. Edge-disjoint paths are paths that do not share any common edges.

Let's construct edge-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1:** J - M - L - F
    Edges used: {(J, M), (M, L), (L, F)}

2.  **Path 2:** J - D - P - O - C - F
    Edges used: {(J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, F)}
    This path uses edges distinct from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3:** J - I - H - G - F
    Edges used: {(J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, F)}
    This path uses edges distinct from Path 1 and Path 2.

4.  **Path 4:** J - P - Y - N - B - E - F
    Edges used: {(J, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, E), (E, F)}
    Let's verify if these edges are distinct from Paths 1, 2, and 3:
    *   (J, P) is distinct from (J, M), (J, D), (J, I).
    *   (P, Y) is distinct from (M, L), (D, P), (I, H). Note that (D,P) is used in Path 2, but (P,Y) is a different edge from P.
    *   (Y, N) is distinct from (L, F), (P, O), (H, G).
    *   (N, B) is distinct from (O, C), (G, F).
    *   (B, E) is distinct from (C, F).
    *   (E, F) is a new edge to F.
    This path is indeed edge-disjoint from Paths 1, 2, and 3.

We have successfully identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between J and F.
The starting edges from J for these paths are (J, M), (J, D), (J, I), and (J, P). These are all the edges connected to J.
Since all edges connected to J are used in these 4 edge-disjoint paths, it is impossible to find a 5th edge-disjoint path starting from J.
Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between J and F is 4.

According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect J from F is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 4.

The cut set that achieves this minimum is the set of all edges directly connected to node J: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, I), (J, P)]. Removing these 4 edges isolates J, thus ensuring no path exists between J and F.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, D), (J, I), (J, P)]

**Analysis of paths from G to I:**

There are two main "channels" or groups of paths from G to I:

**Channel 1: Paths through node H**
*   G - H - I (direct path)
*   G - H - B - C - I
*   G - H - B - C - P - I
*   G - H - B - C - N - O - P - I

**Channel 2: Paths through nodes F, E, M, J (or P, C)**
*   G - F - E - M - J - I
*   G - F - E - M - J - P - I
*   G - F - E - M - J - P - C - I
*   (Note: Paths like G-F-E-H-I are covered by Channel 1 if H is removed, or by Channel 2 if F or E is removed.)

*   **Removing F:** Paths through H (e.g., G-H-I) still exist. Not enough.
*   **Removing K or X:** These nodes are only connected to G and F. Removing them does not disconnect G from I. Not enough.

*   Other nodes (B, N, O, Y, L, D) are further away and clearly not single cut nodes.

Since no single node can disconnect G and I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

**Testing for a two-node cut:**

**Option 1: Remove H (from Channel 1) and E (from Channel 2).**
1.  **Remove H:** All paths from G that go through H are broken. This includes G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I, and G-F-E-H-I.
    After removing H, G is connected to {F, K, X}. I is connected to {J, P, C}.

2.  **Remove E:** Now, consider paths from F. The only way for F to reach I's side (J, P, C) is through E (F-E-M-J...). If E is removed, F cannot reach M, and thus cannot reach J, P, or C.

This cut set [H, E] successfully disconnects G and I.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

First, I will identify vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. Vertex-disjoint paths are paths that do not share any intermediate nodes. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

I can identify the following three vertex-disjoint paths from E to I:
1.  **Path 1:** E - B - C - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
2.  **Path 2:** E - F - G - H - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H}
3.  **Path 3:** E - M - J - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J}

Let's verify that these three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint (excluding the start node E and end node I):
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {B, C}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {F, G, H}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {M, J}.
These three sets are mutually disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect E and I must be at least 3.

Next, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E from I.
Let's consider the nodes directly connected to I: H, C, J, P. Any path from E to I must pass through at least one of these nodes.

Consider removing the nodes {H, C, J}.
Let's check if removing these three nodes disconnects E from I:
*   **Paths through H:** Any path from E to I that uses H (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-F-G-H-I, E-F-X-G-H-I) will be broken by the removal of H.
*   **Paths through C:** Any path from E to I that uses C (e.g., E-B-C-I, E-B-C-O-P-I, E-B-C-N-O-P-I) will be broken by the removal of C.
*   **Paths through J:** Any path from E to I that uses J (e.g., E-M-J-I, E-M-J-P-I, E-L-M-J-I, E-L-M-J-P-I) will be broken by the removal of J.

After removing H, C, and J, the only remaining direct neighbor of I that could potentially connect to E is P. So, if a path still exists, it must pass through P. Let's check all paths from E to P:
*   E - B - C - O - P: This path is broken because C is removed.
*   E - B - C - N - O - P: This path is broken because C is removed.
*   E - M - J - P: This path is broken because J is removed.
*   E - L - M - J - P: This path is broken because J is removed.

Since all paths to P are also broken by the removal of C or J, and all paths through H, C, and J are directly broken, there are no remaining paths from E to I.
Thus, the set {H, C, J} is a cut set of size 3.

Since we established that at least 3 nodes must be removed (from Menger's Theorem) and we found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Another valid cut set of size 3 would be {B, F, M}, as these nodes represent the initial branching points from E towards I.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [H, C, J]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from G.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node N is connected to: Y, O, C, B.
    *   Node G is connected to: H, K, F, X.

2.  **Analyze the structure of the graph to find critical connections:**
    To disconnect N from G, we need to ensure that no path exists between them. Notice that nodes K and X are only connected to G and F. This means that if we can disconnect N from H and F, then N will also be disconnected from K and X (and thus from G). Therefore, the problem reduces to finding a minimum cut that disconnects N from both H and F.

3.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges that connect the "N-side" of the graph to H and F:**
    Let's consider the edges that bridge the gap between the general area of N and the immediate vicinity of G (specifically H and F).
    *   Edges leading directly to H:
        *   (B, H) - connects N's neighbor B to H.
        *   (I, H) - connects node I to H. Node I can be reached from N via C or O-P.
    *   Edges leading directly to F:
        *   (P, F) - connects node P to F. Node P can be reached from N via O, C-I, or B-E.

4.  **Propose a cut set based on these bottleneck edges:**
    A strong candidate for a minimal cut set would be the edges that, if removed, would sever all connections to H and F.
    Let's consider the set of edges: `S = [(B, H), (I, H), (P, F)]`. This set contains 3 edges.

5.  **Verify that removing these 3 edges disconnects N from G:**
    *   **Paths to H:**
        *   Any path from N to H that goes through B (e.g., N-B-H) will be cut by removing (B, H).
        *   Any path from N to H that goes through I (e.g., N-C-I-H, N-O-P-I-H) will be cut by removing (I, H).
        *   Since all paths to H must pass through either B or I, removing (B, H) and (I, H) ensures that N cannot reach H.
    *   **Paths to F:**
        *   Any path from N to F (e.g., N-O-P-F, N-C-I-P-F, N-B-E-P-F) must eventually pass through P and then (P, F). Removing (P, F) ensures that N cannot reach F.

    Since all paths from N to H and all paths from N to F are cut, and K and X are only connected to G and F, N is completely disconnected from G. This cut set of 3 edges is effective.

6.  **Prove that 2 edges are not sufficient (i.e., prove minimality):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect N from G. Let's consider removing any two edges from our proposed cut set `S = [(B, H), (I, H), (P, F)]` and see if a path remains:
    *   **Case 1: Remove (B, H) and (I, H).**
        *   A path to F still exists: N - O - P - F - G. (This path does not use (B,H) or (I,H)).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.
    *   **Case 2: Remove (B, H) and (P, F).**
        *   A path to H still exists: N - C - I - H - G. (This path does not use (B,H) or (P,F)).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.
    *   **Case 3: Remove (I, H) and (P, F).**
        *   A path to H still exists: N - B - H - G. (This path does not use (I,H) or (P,F)).
        *   Therefore, N is still connected to G.

    Since removing any two edges from the set `[(B, H), (I, H), (P, F)]` leaves at least one path between N and G, all three edges are necessary to achieve disconnection. Thus, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths from H to N:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N

    *   From Path 3: (H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

        *   M -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B

3.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut), I will look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between M and B (Menger's Theorem).
    *   **Path 1:** M - E - B
        *   Intermediate nodes: {E}
    *   **Path 2:** M - F - G - B
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, G}
    *   **Path 3:** M - J - P - O - C - B
        *   Intermediate nodes: {J, P, O, C}

    These three paths are vertex-disjoint (meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes).
    *   Path 1 uses E.
    *   Path 2 uses F and G.
    *   Path 3 uses J, P, O, and C.
    Since these sets of intermediate nodes are distinct, we have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect M and B must be at least 3.

4.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 3:** Based on the analysis, I will propose a cut set of 3 nodes and verify if it disconnects M and B. A good candidate set would be nodes that are critical to each of the identified disjoint paths or cover multiple path groups.
    Let's consider the set {E, G, J}.
    *   **Remove E:** This breaks all paths that directly use E to reach B, or use E to reach G and then B.
        *   M -> E -> B (broken)
        *   M -> L -> E -> B (broken)
        *   M -> E -> G -> B (broken)
        *   M -> L -> E -> G -> B (broken)
    *   **Remove G:** This breaks all paths that use G to reach B (either directly or via H). Note that some paths through G might also pass through E, which is also removed.
        *   M -> F -> G -> B (broken)
        *   M -> F -> K -> G -> B (broken)
        *   M -> F -> X -> K -> G -> B (broken)
        *   M -> F -> G -> H -> B (broken)
        *   M -> E -> G -> H -> B (broken by E or G)
        *   M -> L -> E -> G -> H -> B (broken by E or G)
    *   **Remove J:** This breaks all paths that originate from M through J.
        *   M -> J -> I -> B (broken)
        *   M -> J -> I -> C -> B (broken)
        *   M -> J -> P -> I -> B (broken)
        *   M -> J -> P -> I -> C -> B (broken)
        *   M -> J -> P -> O -> C -> B (broken)
        *   M -> J -> P -> O -> N -> B (broken)

5.  **Verify Disconnection with {E, G, J}:**
    *   **Paths starting M -> L:** L is connected to M and E. Since E is removed, no path from M through L can reach B.
    *   **Paths starting M -> F:** F is connected to M, E, X, K, G. Since E and G are removed, any path from M through F would need to pass through E or G (or K/X which lead to G). All such paths are broken.
    *   **Paths starting M -> J:** Since J is removed, no path from M through J can reach B.
    *   **Paths starting M -> E:** Since E is removed, no path from M through E can reach B.

    All possible paths from M to B are broken by removing nodes E, G, and J.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 ({E, G, J}) that successfully disconnects M and B. Therefore, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [E, G, J]

1.  **Initialize BFS:**
    *   Queue: `[(F, [F])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{F}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Current node: F. Path: `[F]`

3.  **Level 1: Explore neighbors of F.**
    *   F is connected to E. Add `(E, [F, E])` to queue. Mark E as visited.
    *   F is connected to G. Add `(G, [F, G])` to queue. Mark G as visited.
    *   F is connected to X. Add `(X, [F, X])` to queue. Mark X as visited.
    *   F is connected to K. Add `(K, [F, K])` to queue. Mark K as visited.
    *   F is connected to M. Add `(M, [F, M])` to queue. Mark M as visited.
    *   None of these neighbors are the target node J.

    *   Queue: `[(E, [F, E]), (G, [F, G]), (X, [F, X]), (K, [F, K]), (M, [F, M])]`
    *   Visited: `{F, E, G, X, K, M}`

4.  **Level 2: Explore neighbors of nodes from Level 1.**
    *   Dequeue `(E, [F, E])`. Neighbors of E: L, M, F, G, B.
        *   L is not visited. Add `(L, [F, E, L])` to queue. Mark L as visited.
        *   M, F, G are already visited.
        *   B is not visited. Add `(B, [F, E, B])` to queue. Mark B as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(G, [F, G])`. Neighbors of G: F, E, H, K, B.
        *   F, E, K, B are already visited.
        *   H is not visited. Add `(H, [F, G, H])` to queue. Mark H as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(X, [F, X])`. Neighbors of X: F, K.
        *   F, K are already visited.
    *   Dequeue `(K, [F, K])`. Neighbors of K: F, X, G, J.
        *   F, X, G are already visited.
        *   J is not visited. Add `(J, [F, K, J])` to queue. **J is the target node!**

    *   Since J has been reached, the shortest path has been found. The path is `[F, K, J]`.
    *   The length of this path is 2 (F-K, K-J).

5.  **Verification:**
    *   Is F directly connected to K? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
    *   Is K directly connected to J? Yes, there is a continuous black line.
    *   The path `[F, K, J]` is valid.
    *   The BFS algorithm guarantees that the first path found to the target node is the shortest.

(Note: Another path of length 2 is `[F, M, J]`. F is connected to M, and M is connected to J. Both are valid shortest paths.)

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N and H.

1.  **Identify the neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, B
    *   Neighbors of H: B, G, I

2.  **Consider a simple cut set:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges connected to it.
    *   To disconnect H, we can remove its incident edges: (B, H), (G, H), (I, H). This would isolate H from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from N. This cut set has 3 edges.
    *   Similarly, to disconnect N, we can remove its incident edges: (N, Y), (N, O), (N, B). This would isolate N from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from H. This cut set also has 3 edges.

    This suggests that the minimum number of edges to remove might be 3. To confirm this is the *minimum*, we need to check if it's possible with fewer edges (e.g., 1 or 2).

3.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least 3.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths from N to H:

    *   **Path 1:** N - B - H
        Edges: `{(N, B), (B, H)}`

    *   **Path 2:** N - O - C - I - H
        Edges: `{(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`
        (This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different edges from N and H, and the intermediate nodes/edges are also distinct from Path 1).

    *   **Path 3:** Now we need a path that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far: `{(N, B), (B, H), (N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`.
        From N, the only remaining unused edge is (N, Y). So, Path 3 must start with N - Y.
        From Y, we can go to O. The edge (Y, O) is available.
        From O, we cannot use (O, C) (used in Path 2) or (N, O) (used in Path 2). We can use (O, P).
        From P, we cannot use (P, I) because (I, H) is used in Path 2. So we must use (P, D).
        From D, we can go to J.
        From J, we can go to M.
        From M, we can go to L or E. Let's try M - L.
        From L, we can go to E.
        From E, we cannot use (E, B) because (B, H) is used in Path 1. So we must use (E, F).
        From F, we can go to X or K. Let's try F - X.
        From X, we can go to G.
        From G, the edge (G, H) is available (not used in Path 1 or Path 2).
        So, **Path 3:** N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - X - G - H
        Edges: `{(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, D), (D, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)}`

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   Path 1: `(N, B), (B, H)`
    *   Path 2: `(N, O), (O, C), (C, I), (I, H)`
    *   Path 3: `(N, Y), (Y, O), (O, P), (P, D), (D, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)`

    Upon inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique. There are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these are 3 edge-disjoint paths.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. We previously identified a cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`. Since we have a cut set of size 3 and the minimum required is at least 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are M (black) and N (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect M and N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node M is connected to: J, E, L.
    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

2.  **Check for a cut set of size 1:**
    To disconnect M and N, we would need to find a single edge whose removal breaks all paths between them.
    *   If we remove (J, M), paths like M-E-H-B-N still exist.
    *   If we remove (E, M), paths like M-J-P-O-N still exist.
    *   If we remove (L, M), paths like M-E-H-B-N still exist.
    *   Similarly, removing any single edge connected to N (e.g., (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N)) would leave other paths intact.
    *   Removing any single "middle" edge (e.g., (H, B), (I, C), (P, O)) would also leave alternative paths.
    Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect M and N. The minimum number of edges is at least 2.

3.  **Explore cut sets of size 2:**
    Let's try to find a set of two edges that, when removed, disconnect M from N.

    *   **Strategy 1: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
        Consider isolating node M. M has three direct connections: (J, M), (E, M), and (L, M).
        *   If we remove (J, M) and (E, M):
            *   M is no longer connected to J.
            *   M is no longer connected to E.
            *   M is still connected to L via the edge (L, M).
            *   Node L is connected to M and E. Since the edge (E, M) has been removed, L cannot serve as a bridge from M to E (or any other part of the graph via E).
            *   Therefore, after removing (J, M) and (E, M), M can only reach L, and L can only reach M. This effectively isolates the component {M, L} from the rest of the graph.
            *   Since N is not in the {M, L} component, M and N are completely disconnected.
            This is a valid cut set of size 2: [(J, M), (E, M)].

        Let's verify if other pairs of edges connected to M would work:
        *   If we remove (J, M) and (L, M): M is still connected to E. Paths like M-E-H-B-N would still exist. This is not a cut set.
        *   If we remove (E, M) and (L, M): M is still connected to J. Paths like M-J-P-O-N would still exist. This is not a cut set.

        So, [(J, M), (E, M)] is a unique cut set of size 2 that isolates M (along with L).

    *   **Strategy 2: Isolate node N.**
        N has four direct connections: (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N).
        *   If we remove (B, N) and (C, N): Paths through O still exist (e.g., M-J-P-O-N).
        *   If we remove (B, N) and (O, N): Paths through C still exist (e.g., M-J-P-C-N).
        *   If we remove (C, N) and (O, N): Paths through B still exist (e.g., M-E-H-B-N).
        Therefore, removing any two direct edges to N is not sufficient to disconnect N from M. At least three edges would be needed to isolate N (e.g., (B, N), (C, N), (O, N)).

    *   **Strategy 3: Find a "middle" cut of size 2.**
        This would involve finding two edges not directly connected to M or N, whose removal breaks all paths. While possible in some graphs, the complexity of paths in this graph makes it harder to identify such a minimal set compared to isolating a node. Given that we've found a cut set of size 2 by isolating M, we don't need to search further for a smaller set.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    We have established that 1 edge is not sufficient. We have found a cut set of 2 edges, [(J, M), (E, M)], which successfully disconnects M from N. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

The final answer is: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, M), (E, M)]

    *   Similarly, if a path goes G-X, it must then go X-F or X-K. If X-F, it passes through F. If X-K, it then must go K-F to leave the {G,K,X} cluster.
    *   Therefore, any path originating from G and moving towards the rest of the graph (and thus towards N) must pass through either node H or node F. Nodes K and X are part of a local cluster with G and F, and any path through K or X eventually leads to F to exit this cluster.

3.  **Identify potential cut nodes:** Based on the observation above, H and F are critical nodes. If both H and F are removed, all paths from G to the rest of the graph (and thus to N) would be severed.

4.  **Test the proposed cut set [H, F]:**
    *   If H is removed, all paths starting G-H-... are broken.
    *   If F is removed, all paths starting G-F-... are broken.
    *   Since all paths from G must pass through either H or F, removing both H and F will completely disconnect G from N.

5.  **Check for a smaller cut set (size 1):**
    *   If only H is removed, paths through F still exist (e.g., G-F-E-I-C-N).
    *   If only F is removed, paths through H still exist (e.g., G-H-B-N).
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

6.  **Confirm with Menger's Theorem (optional but good practice):** To confirm the minimum cut size is 2, I can look for two vertex-disjoint paths between G and N (excluding G and N themselves).
    *   Path 1: G - H - B - N (Intermediate nodes: H, B)
    *   Path 2: G - F - E - I - C - N (Intermediate nodes: F, E, I, C)
    These two sets of intermediate nodes ({H, B} and {F, E, I, C}) are disjoint. This confirms that there are at least two vertex-disjoint paths, meaning the minimum vertex cut size is at least 2.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since a cut set of size 2 ([H, F]) has been found, and it's confirmed that a smaller cut set is not possible, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are N and P. My goal is to disconnect N from P by removing the minimum number of white nodes. I cannot remove N or P.

2.  **Analyze Connections to Target Nodes:**
    *   Nodes directly connected to N: B, C, Y.
    *   Nodes directly connected to P: I, O, J, D. (Node D is only connected to J and P, and J is connected to P, so D does not provide an alternative path to N if J is removed).

3.  **Explore Paths and Potential Bottlenecks:**
    To disconnect N and P, I must sever all paths between them. I will look for "bottleneck" nodes or sets of nodes that all paths must pass through.

4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove any single node, there are clearly multiple alternative paths. For example, removing node E still leaves paths like N-C-I-P, N-Y-O-P, N-B-C-O-P, N-B-G-H-I-P, N-B-C-I-P, etc. Thus, a single node removal is not sufficient.

5.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing two nodes that are direct neighbors of N:
        *   If I remove [B, C]: Path N-Y-O-P still exists.
        *   If I remove [B, Y]: Paths N-C-I-P and N-C-O-P still exist.
        *   If I remove [C, Y]: Paths through B (e.g., N-B-E-L-M-J-P, N-B-E-G-H-I-P) still exist.
    *   Consider removing two nodes that are direct neighbors of P:
        *   If I remove [I, O]: Paths through J (e.g., N-B-E-L-M-J-P, N-B-E-F-M-J-P) still exist.
        *   If I remove [I, J]: Paths through O (e.g., N-Y-O-P, N-C-O-P, N-B-C-O-P) still exist.
        *   If I remove [O, J]: Paths through I (e.g., N-C-I-P, N-B-C-I-P, N-B-E-G-H-I-P) still exist.
    *   Since removing any pair of direct neighbors of N or P does not disconnect them, it suggests that a cut set of size 2 is likely not sufficient.

6.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths:**
    To confirm the minimum number of nodes, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and P (paths that share no intermediate nodes).
    *   **Path 1:** N - C - I - P (Intermediate nodes: C, I)
    *   **Path 2:** N - Y - O - P (Intermediate nodes: Y, O)
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - L - M - J - P (Intermediate nodes: B, E, L, M, J)

    These three paths are indeed vertex-disjoint (they do not share any intermediate nodes). According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 'k' vertex-disjoint paths between two nodes, then at least 'k' nodes must be removed to disconnect them. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I, without removing E or I themselves.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (the vertex connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's identify vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. Two paths are vertex-disjoint if they do not share any common intermediate nodes (nodes other than the start and end nodes, E and I).

1.  **Path 1: E - M - J - P - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}

2.  **Path 2: E - F - K - G - H - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G, H}
    *   Comparing with Path 1's intermediate nodes {M, J, P}, there are no common nodes. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are vertex-disjoint.

3.  **Path 3: E - L - N - C - I**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {L, N, C}
    *   Comparing with Path 1's intermediate nodes {M, J, P}, there are no common nodes.
    *   Comparing with Path 2's intermediate nodes {F, K, G, H}, there are no common nodes.
    *   So, Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are all mutually vertex-disjoint.

Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between E and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect E and I is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
The nodes P, C, and H are the only direct neighbors of node I. If I remove these three nodes, node I will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node E.
Let's verify this cut set:
*   Removing P breaks Path 1 (E - M - J - P - I).
*   Removing C breaks Path 3 (E - L - N - C - I).
*   Removing H breaks Path 2 (E - F - K - G - H - I).

Since P, C, and H are the only nodes directly connected to I, removing them ensures that no path can reach I from any other node in the graph, including E. Therefore, [P, C, H] is a valid cut set.

Since I found three vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3. And I found a cut set of size 3 ([P, C, H]). This confirms that the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, C, H]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H.

First, I identify all direct connections to the target nodes:
*   Edges connected to E: (E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (E, L), (E, M)
*   Edges connected to H: (H, E), (H, F), (H, G), (H, B), (H, I)

To disconnect E and H, I need to ensure that no path exists between them. A straightforward way to achieve this is to isolate one of the nodes by removing all its incident edges.

1.  **Consider isolating node H:**
    If I remove all edges connected to H, it will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from E. The edges connected to H are (H, E), (H, F), (H, G), (H, B), and (H, I). This set contains 5 edges. Removing these 5 edges guarantees disconnection.

2.  **Consider isolating node E:**
    Similarly, if I remove all edges connected to E, it will be completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from H. The edges connected to E are (E, H), (E, F), (E, G), (E, L), and (E, M). This set also contains 5 edges. Removing these 5 edges guarantees disconnection.

Now, I need to determine if a smaller number of edges (e.g., 4, 3, or 2) can achieve the same disconnection.

*   **Can 2 edges disconnect E and H?**
    Let's try removing any two edges. For example, if I remove (E, H) and (H, F), there are still many paths, such as E-G-H, E-L-M-J-I-H, etc. It's clear that 2 edges are not enough.

*   **Can 3 edges disconnect E and H?**
    Let's assume we remove the direct edge (E, H). We now need to cut all other paths between E and H using 2 more edges.
    The remaining paths from E to H can be broadly categorized into two groups:
    *   **Group 1 (via F/G cluster):** Paths like E-F-H, E-G-H, E-F-K-G-H, E-G-X-F-H, etc. These paths use nodes F and G as intermediaries. To cut all paths through F and G, we would need to remove at least two edges, for example, (H, F) and (H, G).
    *   **Group 2 (via L/M/J/I/P/D/C/B/N/O/Y cluster):** Paths like E-L-M-J-I-H, E-M-J-P-O-C-B-H, etc. These paths use nodes B and I as intermediaries to reach H. To cut all paths through B and I, we would need to remove at least two edges, for example, (H, B) and (H, I).
    If we remove (E, H) (1 edge), we still need to cut paths from Group 1 AND Group 2. This would require at least 2 edges for Group 1 and at least 2 edges for Group 2. So, 1 + 2 + 2 = 5 edges would be needed in this scenario. This shows that 3 edges are not enough.

*   **Can 4 edges disconnect E and H?**
    Following the logic from the 3-edge analysis:
    If we remove (E, H) (1 edge), we have 3 more edges to cut.
    As established, we need at least 2 edges to cut Group 1 paths (e.g., (H, F), (H, G)) and at least 2 edges to cut Group 2 paths (e.g., (H, B), (H, I)). Since 2 + 2 = 4, we would need 4 edges *in addition* to (E, H) to cut all these paths. This means a total of 1 + 4 = 5 edges.
    Therefore, 4 edges are not sufficient to disconnect E and H.

Since 4 edges are not enough, and 5 edges (by isolating either E or H) are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 5.

One possible cut set is to remove all edges incident to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 5, Cut Set: [(H, E), (H, F), (H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 4
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are F and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect F and J, without removing F or J themselves.

First, I will identify all possible paths from F to J.
The immediate neighbors of F are X, K, G, and E.
The immediate neighbors of J are L, M, I, P, and D.

Let's analyze the "entry points" from F to the rest of the graph.
- Node X is connected to F, K, G.
- Node K is connected to F, X, G.
- Node G is connected to F, X, K, H.
- Node E is connected to F, M, L, I, B.

Any path starting from F must take one of these initial steps: F-X, F-K, F-G, or F-E.
- If a path starts F-X, it must then go to K or G (F-X-K or F-X-G). If it goes F-X-K, K is connected to F, X, G. So it must eventually go to G (F-X-K-G).
- Similarly, if a path starts F-K, it must then go to X or G (F-K-X or F-K-G). If it goes F-K-X, X is connected to F, K, G. So it must eventually go to G (F-K-X-G).
Therefore, any path originating from F that does not go through E must eventually pass through G.

This means all paths from F to J can be categorized into two main groups:
1. Paths that pass through node E.
2. Paths that pass through node G (this includes paths starting F-X-G or F-K-G).

Let's verify if removing a single node can disconnect F and J:
- If I remove E: Paths like F-G-H-I-J would still exist.
- If I remove G: Paths like F-E-I-J would still exist.
- If I remove H: Paths like F-E-I-J would still exist.
- If I remove I: Paths like F-E-M-J would still exist.
- If I remove M: Paths like F-E-I-J would still exist.
- If I remove P: Paths like F-E-I-J would still exist.
- Removing any other single node (X, K, B, C, N, O, L, D, Y) would also leave at least one path intact.
Thus, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect F and J. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

Now, let's consider removing two nodes.
Based on the observation that all paths must pass through either E or G, removing both E and G should disconnect F and J.

Neighbors of J: D, P, M, I
Neighbors of E: L, B, F, M

I will systematically identify all distinct "channels" or "bottlenecks" through which paths from J can reach E.

**Channel 1: Paths through node M**
*   J has a direct connection to M: (J, M).
*   M has connections to E: (M, E) and (M, L) where L connects to E via (L, E).
*   M also connects to O, which leads to B and then E.
    *   Paths: J-M-E, J-M-L-E, J-M-O-N-B-E, J-M-O-Y-N-B-E, J-M-O-C-B-E.
    *   To cut all paths originating from J through M, removing the edge (J, M) is the most efficient single cut for this channel.

**Channel 2: Paths through node B**
*   Many paths from J eventually converge to B before reaching E via (B, E).
    *   Paths from J via D, P, O, N, C, Y: J-D-P-O-N-B-E, J-D-P-O-Y-N-B-E, J-D-P-O-C-B-E, J-P-O-N-B-E, J-P-O-Y-N-B-E, J-P-O-C-B-E.
    *   Paths from J via I, H, C: J-I-H-B-E, J-I-C-B-E, J-I-C-O-N-B-E, J-I-C-O-Y-N-B-E.
    *   All these paths share the final edge (B, E) to reach E. Removing (B, E) would cut all of them.

**Channel 3: Paths through node F**
*   Paths from J via I, H, X, K, G eventually converge to F before reaching E via (F, E).
    *   Paths: J-I-H-X-K-F-E, J-I-H-X-G-F-E, J-I-H-G-F-E.
    *   All these paths share the final edge (F, E) to reach E. Removing (F, E) would cut all of them.

Based on this analysis, I propose the following cut set:
1.  **(J, M):** This edge cuts off all paths that start from J and go through M (J-M-E, J-M-L-E, J-M-O-...). After removing (J,M), J cannot reach M, and therefore cannot reach E via M or L (since L is only connected to M and E).
2.  **(B, E):** This edge cuts off all paths that reach E via node B. This includes paths from J through D, P, O, N, Y, C, I, H.
3.  **(F, E):** This edge cuts off all paths that reach E via node F. This includes paths from J through I, H, X, K, G.

Let's verify that these three edges are sufficient and necessary:
*   **Sufficiency:**
    *   After removing (J, M), J cannot reach M. Thus, no path J-...-M-E or J-...-M-L-E exists.
    *   After removing (B, E), no path J-...-B-E exists.
    *   After removing (F, E), no path J-...-F-E exists.
    *   Since E's only remaining connections are to L, B, F, and M, and we've ensured J cannot reach M, B, or F (or L via M), there are no remaining paths from J to E.

*   **Necessity (Can we do it with fewer than 3 edges?):**
    *   If (J, M) is NOT removed, then J-M-E is a path.
    *   If (B, E) is NOT removed (and (J,M) is removed), then J-I-H-B-E is a path.
    *   If (F, E) is NOT removed (and (J,M) is removed), then J-I-H-G-F-E is a path.
    *   Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path open, all three edges are necessary.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
